Renormalization of Unicritical Diffeomorphisms of the Disk Thanks: The first author was partially supported by the ERC project 692925 – NUHGD. The second author was partly supported by the NSF, the Hagler and Clay Fellowships, the Institute for Theoretical Studies at ETH (Zurich), and SLMSI (formerly MSRI Berkeley). The fourth author was partially supported by SLMSI, Institut Mittag-Leffler, and the thematic programs ‘Topological, smooth and holomorphic dynamics, ergodic theory, fractals’ of the Simons Foundation Award No. 663281 for IMPAN
Sylvain Crovisier, Mikhail Lyubich, Enrique Pujals, Jonguk Yang

Abstract
. We introduce a class of infinitely renormalizable unicritical diffeomorphisms of the disk (with a non-degenerate “critical point”). In this class of dynamical systems, we show that under renormalization, maps eventually become Hénon-like, and then converge super-exponentially fast to the space of one-dimensional unimodal maps. We also completely characterize the local geometry of every stable and center manifolds that exist in these systems. The theory is based upon a quantitative reformulation of the Oseledets-Pesin theory yielding a unicritical structure of the maps in question comprising regular Pesin boxes co-existing with “critical tunnels” and “valuable crescents”. In forthcoming notes we will show that infinitely renormalizable perturbative Hénon-like maps of bounded type belong to our class.



1.
Introduction

Critical points play a fundamental role in one-dimensional dynamics as a natural source of non-linearity. They allow even very simple maps to have extremely rich and fascinatingly complicated dynamical behaviors.

The simplest setting for studying the dynamical properties of critical points is the class of unimodal maps. A
unimodal map
is a
C^{2}
-map
f:I\to I
on an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
with a unique critical point
c_{0}\in I
. In this paper, we always assume that
c_{0}
is of
quadratic type
:
f^{\prime}(c_{0})=0
and
f^{\prime\prime}(c_{0})\neq 0
. We say that
f
is
normalized
if
c_{0}=0
and
f(c_{0})=1
. Let
r\geq 2
be an integer. The space of normalized
C^{r}
-unimodal maps is denoted
\mathfrak{U}^{r}
.

The model examples of unimodal maps are given by the
quadratic family
:

\mathfrak{Q}:=\{f_{a}(x):=x^{2}+a\;|\;a\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The study of this family has led to some incredibly deep mathematics that spans over two decades of research by numerous authors. At the heart of this topic lies the celebrated renormalization theory of unimodal maps completed by Sullivan, McMullen, Lyubich and Avila.

There have been several works that extend this theory to a higher dimensional setting (see e.g.
[
CoEK
]
,
[
GvST
]
and
[
dCLM
]
). However, the limitation of these approaches is that they are all based on perturbative methods (relying on the robustness of the hyperbolic one-dimensional renormalization operator). Hence, they only apply to systems that are sufficiently close to the space of one-dimensional unimodal maps.

The main goal of this paper is to develop a non-perturbative renormalization theory of “unicritical” diffeomorphisms in dimension two. The most immediate challenge is to identify what being “unicritical” should mean in this context. Drawing motivations from the one-dimensional and the perturbative two-dimensional cases, we define a
critical point
as a point whose local stable and center manifolds are well-defined and form a quadratic tangency. Clearly, any invariant set that feature such a point cannot be uniformly partially hyperbolic. However, if the failure of uniform regularity is restricted to a slow exponentially shrinking neighborhood of the backward orbit of the critical point, then we say that the map is
unicritical
. One may think of this condition as a relaxation of uniform partial hyperbolicity that allows for the existence of a tangency between stable and center manifolds. The main result of this paper is that renormalizations of two-dimensional unicritical diffeomorphisms converge super-exponentially fast to the space of one-dimensional unimodal maps.

1.1.
Hénon-like maps

The two-dimensional analog of the quadratic family is given by the
Hénon family
:

\mathfrak{H}:=\{F_{a,b}(x,y):=(x^{2}+a-by,x)\;|\;a,b\in\mathbb{R}\}.

We identify the degenerate Hénon map
F_{a,0}
with
f_{a}
.
This way,
{\mathfrak{H}}
can be viewed as a two-dimensional extension of the quadratic family.

As in the one-dimensional case, we would like to consider Hénon maps as the model example of a more general class of maps. The simplest and the most obvious generalization is given by Hénon-like maps. Let
V=I\times I\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism
F:V\to F(V)\Subset V
is
Hénon-like
if it is of the form

F(x,y)=(f(x,y),x)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt(x,y)\in V,

where for all
y\in I
, the map
x\mapsto f(x,y)

is unimodal.
We say that
F
is a
normalized
Hénon-like map if
0\in V
, and
f(\cdot,0)
is a normalized unimodal map. The space of normalized
C^{r}
-Hénon-like maps is denoted
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}
.

For
\beta\in(0,1]
, we say that
F
is
\beta
-thin (in
C^{r}
)
if

\|\operatorname{Jac}F\|=\|\partial_{y}f\|_{C^{r-1}}<\beta.

The space of
\beta
-thin maps in
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}
is denoted
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}_{\beta}
. We say that
F\in{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}_{\beta}
is a
perturbative Hénon-like map
if
\beta\ll 1
.

For any 1D map
f:I\to I
, define a degenerate 2D map
\iota(f):V\to V
by

\iota(f)(x,y):=(f(x),x).

(1.1)

We denote
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}_{0}:=\iota({\mathfrak{U}}^{r})
.

1.2.
Renormalizability

Let
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega
be a dissipative
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism defined on a Jordan domain
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
(i.e.
\Omega
is a topological disk whose boundary is a topological circle). We say that
F
is
renormalizable
if there exists an
R
-periodic Jordan subdomain
\mathcal{D}\Subset\Omega
for some integer
R>1
:

F^{i}(\mathcal{D})\cap\mathcal{D}=\varnothing\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq i<R,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptF^{R}(\mathcal{D})\Subset\mathcal{D}.

The map
F
is
infinitely renormalizable
if there exist a nested sequence of of Jordan domains and an increasing sequence of natural numbers

\Omega=:\mathcal{D}^{0}\Supset\mathcal{D}^{1}\Supset\ldots\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt1=:R_{0}<R_{1}<\ldots

such that for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the domain
\mathcal{D}^{n}
is
R_{n}
-periodic, and

r_{n-1}:=R_{n}/R_{n-1}\geq 2.

We refer to
\mathcal{D}^{n}
as the
n
th renormalization domain
. The primary set of interest is the
renormalization limit set
, defined as

\Lambda_{F}:=\bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigcup_{i=0}^{R_{n}-1}F^{i}(\mathcal{D}^{n}).

The
n
th pre-renormalization of
F
is the first return map to the domain
\mathcal{D}^{n}
:

p\mathcal{R}^{n}(F):=F^{R_{n}}|_{\mathcal{D}^{n}}.

Let
\Psi^{n}:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Omega^{n}
be an appropriately defined “rescaling map” (non-linear in general) that turns
\mathcal{D}^{n}
into a domain
\Omega^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of some “standard size.”
The
n
th renormalization of
F
is then:

\mathcal{R}^{n}(F):=\Psi^{n}\circ p\mathcal{R}^{n}(F)\circ(\Psi^{n})^{-1}.

Since
F
is assumed to be dissipative:

\|\operatorname{Jac}F\|\leq\lambda<1,

a heuristic computation shows that

\|\operatorname{Jac}\mathcal{R}^{n}(F)\|\approx\|\operatorname{Jac}F^{R_{n}}\|\leq\lambda^{R_{n}}\rightarrow 0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{as}\hskip 14.22636ptn\to\infty.

Thus, one expects that under renormalization, the dissipative two-dimensional map
F
becomes more and more one-dimensional. Our main goal is to rigorously establish a sufficiently stronger version of this statement for the class of maps that we will introduce below.

1.3.
Mild dissipativity

The map
F
is
mildly dissipative
if for any ergodic measure
\mu
(not supported on a hyperbolic sink), the strong-stable manifold
W^{ss}(p)
of
\mu
-almost every point
p\in\Omega
is
proper
in the following sense: if
W_{\Omega}^{ss}(p)
is the connected component of
W^{ss}(p)\cap\Omega
containing
p
, then
\overline{W^{ss}_{\Omega}(p)}
is a Jordan arc whose endpoints are contained in
\partial\Omega
. See Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Proper strong-stable manifold
W^{ss}(p)
in
\Omega
.

All Hénon maps
F_{a,b}
with
|b|<1/4
are known to be mildly dissipative (see
[
DL
]
and
[
CP
]
). In
[
CPT
]
, Crovisier-Pujals-Tresser give an explicit description of the topological structure of mildly dissipative diffeomorphisms of the disk with zero topological entropy. Specifically, they show that any such map must either be infinitely renormalizable (of eventually period-doubling type), or is a generalized Morse-Smale system.

1.4.
Unique ergodicity

Let
F
be infinitely renormalizable. Suppose that its renormalization limit set
\Lambda_{F}
has a unique invariant measure
\mu
(this is always true if
F
is mildly dissipative, see Proposition
8.2
). The Lyapunov exponents of
F
with respect to
\mu
are
0
and
\log\lambda_{F}<0
for some
\lambda_{F}\in(0,1)
(see Proposition
8.3
). By the Oseledets theorem, the tangent space at
\mu
-a.e. point
p\in\Lambda_{F}
splits as
E^{ss}_{p}\oplus E^{c}_{p}
, so that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|DF^{n}|_{E_{p}^{ss}}\|=\log\lambda_{F}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|DF^{-n}|_{E_{p}^{c}}\|=0.

(1.2)

Since
F
is
C^{r}
with
r>1
, it follows from (
1.2
) and the unique ergodicity that

p
has
C^{r}
-smooth strong-stable and center manifolds
W^{ss}(p)
and
W^{c}(p)
respectively (see Subsection
3.6
and Proposition
4.6
). The former is tangent to
E^{ss}_{p}
and the latter to
E^{c}_{p}
. The center manifold
W^{c}(p)
is not uniquely defined. However, its
C^{r}
-jet at
p
is unique (see Proposition
3.20
).

1.5.
Regular unicriticality

Understanding the dynamics of smooth maps of the interval is centered around the analysis of their critical points. Therefore, when studying smooth maps in dimension two, it is natural to try and identify the “critical sets” of these maps that play a similar role.

This strategy is implemented in the landmark paper of Benedicks-Carleson
[
BCa
]
to prove the existence of strange attractors for a positive measure set of Hénon maps. In their setting, the critical set is defined as a certain set of tangencies between stable and unstable manifolds obtained as the limit of an inductive procedure. This topic has since been further developed by numerous authors, including Wang-Young
[
WYo
]
, Palis-Yoccoz
[
PaYo
]
and Berger
[
Be
]
.

A much more general (albeit less explicit) way to identify the critical set is to characterize it as the obstruction locus for dominated splitting (see Definition
5.1
). This idea is explored by Pujals-Rodriguez Hertz in
[
PR
]
, and Crovisier-Pujals in
[
CP2
]
.

In our paper, we are mainly interested in two-dimensional maps that are infinitely renormalizable. It turns out that such maps can be
unicritical
—the critical set can consist of a single point. The notion of the critical set we use incorporates aspects from the two aforementioned bodies of work. The novelty of our approach is the emphasis on the regularity of points that are not “too close” to the critical orbit. Since the critical orbit is expected to be dense in the renormalization limit set, this property is rather delicate to formulate. The rigorous definitions are given below.

Let
F
be an infinitely renormalizable map as considered in Subsection
1.4
.

Definition 1.1
.

The orbit of a point
p\in\Lambda_{F}
is
regular critical
if
p
admits a direction

E^{*}_{p}:=E^{ss}_{p}=E^{c}_{p}

that satisfies both conditions in (
1.2
).
The criticality is
quadratic
if, additionally,

W^{ss}(p)
and
W^{c}(p)
have a quadratic tangency at
p
.

Definition 1.2
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
;
L\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we say that the point
p
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular
if
it admits a direction
E_{p}
such that

\|DF^{n}|_{E_{p}}|\|\leq L\lambda_{F}^{\varepsilon n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for each}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq N.

(1.3)

For
t>0
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we denote the ball

\mathbb{D}_{p}(t):=\{q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\;|\;\operatorname{dist}(q,p)<t\}.

Definition 1.3
.

For
0<\varepsilon<\delta<1
, we say that
F
is
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-regularly unicritical on the limit set
\Lambda_{F}
if the following conditions hold.

i)

There is a regular quadratic critical orbit point
c_{1}\in\Lambda_{F}
(referred to as the
critical value
).

ii)

For all
t>0
, there exists
L(t)\geq 1
such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, if

p\in\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{D}_{F^{-n}(c_{1})}(t\lambda_{F}^{\varepsilon n}),

(1.4)

then
p
is
N
-times forward
(L(t),\delta)
-regular.

When
\delta
and
\varepsilon
are implicit, we simply say that
F
is
regularly unicritical on
\Lambda_{F}
.

Remark 1.4
.

By Proposition
6.5
, the measure of the set of points satisfying (
1.4
) for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
goes to
1
as
t
goes to
0
. This shows that the condition imposed by Definition
1.3
ii) is not vacuous. Moreover, by Proposition
9.22
, a regularly unicritical system has a unique critical orbit as the name implies.

Remark 1.5
.

The analog of Definition
1.3
holds in the 1D case. See Appendix
A
.

Remark 1.6
.

In the 1D case, an important motivation for the introduction of unimodal maps is that a pre-renormalization of a unimodal map is again unimodal. Observe that in the 2D case, regular unicriticality is likewise preserved under pre-renormalization.

Remark 1.7
.

In
[
CLPY2
]
, we will show that any infinitely renormalizable
C^{2}
-diffeomorphisms of bounded type whose renormalizations are eventually contained in a
C^{1}
-compact subset of
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{2}
is regularly unicritical on its renormalization limit set.

1.6.
Statements of the main results

Consider a Hénon-like map
G:V\to V
defined on a square
V=I\times I\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing
0
. Denote the critical point of
g(\cdot):=G(\cdot,0)
by
c\in I
. Let
\mathbb{S}_{G}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the affine scaling map given by

\mathbb{S}_{G}(x,y):=|g(c)-c|^{-1}(x-c,y).

Observe that
\mathbb{S}_{G}\circ G\circ\mathbb{S}_{G}^{-1}
is normalized.

Main Theorem
.

There exist universal constants
\varepsilon_{0},\alpha\in(0,1)
such that the following holds.
For an integer
r\geq 2
, let
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a mildly dissipative, infinitely renormalizable
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism with
renormalization limit set

\Lambda_{F}:=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{i=0}^{R_{n}-1}F^{i}(\mathcal{D}^{n})

Suppose
F
is
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{1/\alpha})
-regularly unicritical on
\Lambda_{F}
for some
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
with the critical value
c_{1}
. Then for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, there exists a
R_{n}
-periodic quadrilateral
\mathcal{V}^{n}
containing
c_{1}
, and a
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism

\Psi^{n}:(\mathcal{V}^{n},c_{1})\to(V^{n},0)

from
\mathcal{V}^{n}
to a square
V^{n}=I^{n}\times I^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that the following properties hold.

i)

We have
\Lambda_{F}\cap\mathcal{D}^{n}\Subset\mathcal{V}^{n}\Subset\mathcal{V}^{n-1}
.

ii)

We have

\|(\Psi^{n})^{\pm 1}\|_{C^{r}}=O(1)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\|\Psi^{n}-\Psi^{n-1}|_{\mathcal{V}^{n}}\|_{C^{r}}<\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon^{\alpha})R_{n}}.

iii)

Define the
n
th pre-renormalization and the renormalization of
F
as

F_{n}:=\Psi^{n}\circ F^{R_{n}}|_{\mathcal{V}^{n}}\circ(\Psi^{n})^{-1}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{R}^{n}(F):=\mathbb{S}_{F_{n}}\circ F_{n}\circ\mathbb{S}_{F_{n}}^{-1}

respectively. Then
\mathcal{R}^{n}(F)
is a
\delta_{n}
-thin
C^{r}
-Hénon-like map with
\delta_{n}<\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon^{\alpha})R_{n}}
.

As a prerequisite for the proof of the Main Theorem, we obtain an extremely detailed understanding of the regularity of
F
at every point in
\Lambda_{F}
(not just on unspecified sets of large or full measure). As a result, we are also able to explicitly describe the local geometry of every strong stable and center manifolds that exist in the system. See Theorem
2.1
and the discussion in Subsection
2.2
.

In a sequel to this paper (
[
CLPY2
]
), we prove
a priori
bounds for the class of bounded type infinitely renormalizable
C^{r}
-diffeomorphisms of the disk that satisfy the conclusion of the Main Theorem. As a consequence, we have the following corollaries for the map
F
.

Corollary 1.8
.

Suppose
F
is of bounded type. Then the Hausdorff dimension of
\Lambda_{F}
is strictly less than
1
. In particular,
\Lambda_{F}
is totally disconnected and minimal.

Corollary 1.9
.

Suppose
F
is of bounded type and at least
C^{4}
. Then the sequence of renormalizations of
F
given in the Main Theorem converge exponentially fast to a compact subset of
{\mathfrak{H}\mathfrak{L}}^{r}_{0}
that can be identified with the renormalization horseshoe of 1D unimodal maps.

1.7.
The boundary of chaos in the Hénon family

For
\beta\in(0,1]
, denote

{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}:=\{F_{a,b}\in{\mathfrak{H}}\;|\;b\in[0,\beta)\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{H}}_{0}:=\{F_{a,0}\in{\mathfrak{H}}\}.

Denote by
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
the set of all maps in
{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
that have zero topological entropy and can be approximated by Hénon maps with positive entropy (note that
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}\subsetneq\partial{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
). We refer to
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
as the
boundary of chaos in

{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
. If
F_{a,b}\in\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{1/4}
, then
F_{a,b}
is mildly dissipative and has zero entropy. Hence
F_{a,b}
is infinitely renormalizable by
[
CPT
]
.

A priori, the boundary of chaos could be a highly pathological (disconnected) continuum. However, in
[
dCLM
]
, de Carvalho-Lyubich-Martens characterize this set in the perturbative case (
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\delta}
with
\delta\ll 1
) in the following way.

In the quadratic family, the transition from zero to positive entropy happens precisely at the limit
f_{a_{*}}
of period-doubling bifurcations. Moreover, under renormalization,
\mathcal{R}^{n}(f_{a_{*}})
converges to some universal map
f_{*}\in{\mathfrak{U}}^{r}
as
n\to\infty
. Since
{\mathfrak{H}}_{0}
can be identified as the embedding of the quadratic family under
\iota
(see (
1.1
)), we have
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{0}=\{F_{a_{*},0}=\iota(f_{a_{*}})\}
, and
\mathcal{R}^{n}(F_{a_{*},0})
converges to some universal degenerate
0
-Hénon-like map
F_{*}=\iota(f_{*})
as
n\to\infty
. By the hyperbolicity of renormalization, it follows that there is a real analytic curve

\{F^{*}_{b}:=F_{a_{*}(b),b}\;|\;b\in[0,\delta)\}

of infinitely renormalizable Hénon maps of period-doubling type extending from
F^{*}_{0}=F_{a_{*},0}
such that
\mathcal{R}^{n}(F^{*}_{b})\to F_{*}
as
n\to\infty
. De Carvalho-Lyubich-Martens show that this curve coincides with
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\delta}
.

We conclude this subsection with the following motivating open problem.

Open Problem
.

Characterize the boundary of chaos
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{\beta}
for some definite non-perturbative value of
\beta\in(0,1]
.

Conjecturally,
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}^{0}_{1}
consists of infinitely renormalizable Hénon maps that converge to the period-doubling renormalization fixed point
F_{*}
under renormalization. Note that by Remark
1.7
, all such maps must be regularly unicritical on its renormalization limit set. If the conjeture holds, then one would be able to conclude that
\partial^{1}{\mathfrak{H}}_{1}
is a piecewise analytic curve. Towards this open problem, we state the following corollary of the Main Theorem and Corollary
1.9
.

Corollary 1.10
.

Let
r\geq 4
be an integer. Consider a
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega
defined on a Jordan domain
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Suppose
F
is mildly dissipative, infinitely renormalizable and regularly unicritical on its renormalization limit set. If
F
has zero topological entropy, then
F
is in the boundary of chaos. More precisely, in any neighborhood of
F
in the
C^{r}
-topology, there exists a diffeomorphism with positive topological entropy.

1.8.
Conventions

Unless otherwise specified, we adopt the following conventions.

Any diffeomorphism on a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is assumed to be orientation-preserving. The projective tangent space at a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is denoted by
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
.

We typically denote constants by
K\geq 1
,
k>0
(and less frequently
C\geq 1
,
c>0
).
Given a number
\kappa>0
, we use
\bar{\kappa}
to denote any number that satisfy

\kappa<\bar{\kappa}<C\kappa^{D}

for some uniform constants
C>1
and
D>1
(if
\kappa>1
) or
D\in(0,1)
(if
\kappa<1
). We allow
\bar{\kappa}
to absorb any uniformly bounded coefficient or power. So for example, if
\bar{\kappa}>1
, then we may write

\text{``}\;\;10\bar{\kappa}^{5}=\bar{\kappa}\;\;\text{''}.

Similarly, we use
\underline{\kappa}
to denote any number that satisfy

c\kappa^{d}<\underline{\kappa}<\kappa

for some uniform constants
c\in(0,1)
and
d\in(0,1)
(if
\kappa>1
) or
d>1
(if
\kappa<1
). As before, we allow
\underline{\kappa}
to absorb any uniformly bounded coefficient or power. So for example, if
\underline{\kappa}>1
, then we may write

\text{``}\;\;\tfrac{1}{3}\underline{\kappa}^{1/4}=\underline{\kappa}\;\;\text{''}.

These notations apply to any positive real number: e.g.
{\bar{\varepsilon}}>\varepsilon
,
\underline{\delta}<\delta
,
{\bar{L}}>L
, etc.

Note that
\bar{\kappa}
can be much larger than
\kappa
(similarly,
\underline{\kappa}
can be much smaller than
\kappa
). Sometimes, we may avoid the
\bar{\kappa}
or
\underline{\kappa}
notation when indicating numbers that are somewhat or very close to the original value of
\kappa
. For example, if
\kappa\in(0,1)
is a small number, then we may denote
\kappa^{\prime}:=(1-\bar{\kappa})\kappa
. Then
\underline{\kappa}\ll\kappa^{\prime}<\kappa
.

For any set
X_{m}\subset\Omega
with a numerical index
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote

X_{m+l}:=F^{l}(X_{m})

for all
l\in\mathbb{Z}
for which the right-hand side is well-defined. Similarly, for any direction
E_{p_{m}}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{m}}
at a point
p_{m}\in\Omega
, we denote

E_{p_{m+l}}:=DF^{l}(E_{p_{m}}).

We use
n,m,i,j
to denote integers (and less frequently
l,k
). Typically (but not always),
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. We sometimes use
l>0
for positive geometric quantities (such as length). The letter
i
is never the imaginary number.

We typically use
N,M
to indicate fixed integers (often related to variables
n,m
).

We use calligraphic font
\mathcal{U},\mathcal{T},\mathcal{I},
etc, for objects in the phase space and regular fonts
U,T,I,
etc, for corresponding objects in the linearized/uniformized coordinates. A notable exception is for the invariant manifolds
W^{ss},W^{c}
.

We use
p,q
to indicate points in the phase space, and
z,w
for points in linearized/uniformized coordinates.

2.
Presentation of the Main Concepts and Arguments

We now present dynamical and geometrical features of unicritical diffeomorphisms that are developed in the following sections, and explain key concepts for the proofs.

Let
r\geq 2
be an integer. Consider a mildly dissipative, infinitely renormalizable
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega
of a Jordan domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
which is
(\varepsilon,\underline{\varepsilon})
-regularly unicritical on its renormalization limit set

\Lambda:=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{i=0}^{R_{n}-1}F^{i}(\mathcal{D}^{n})

Let
0
and
\log\lambda<0
be the Lyapunov exponents of
F
with respect to the unique invariant probability measure
\mu
on
\Lambda
.

2.1.
Regular blocks

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be a constant such that
{\bar{\varepsilon}}<1
. We may assume without loss of generality that
F
is sufficiently homogeneous (see Section
4
).

For
L\geq 1
, we say that
p\in\Lambda
is
(infinitely) forward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular
if it has a strong-stable direction
E^{ss}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
such that

\|DF^{n}|_{E_{p}^{ss}}\|<L\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all}\hskip 14.22636ptn\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.1)

Similarly,
p
is
(infinitely) backward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular
if it has a center direction
E^{c}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
such that

\|DF^{-n}|_{E_{p}^{c}}\|<L\lambda^{-\varepsilon n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all}\hskip 14.22636ptn\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.2)

Lastly, we say that
p
is
Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular
if (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) hold, and
\measuredangle(E^{ss}_{p},E^{c}_{p})>1/L
. The constants
L
and
\varepsilon
are referred to as a
regularity factor
and a
marginal exponent
respectively.

If
p
is forward/backward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular, then the forward/backward iterates of
F
can be nearly linearized in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{p}
of
p
, called a
regular neighborhood at
p
, with
\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{U}_{p})=1/{\bar{L}}
(see Theorem
3.6
). If
p
is Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular, then the local linearization can be done for iterates of
F
going in both directions (see Theorem
3.6
).

The sets of all points
p\in\Lambda
such that
p
is infinitely forward, backward and Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular are referred to as the
forward, backward
and
Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular blocks
, and are denoted by
\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}
,
\Lambda^{-}_{L,\varepsilon}
and
\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon}
respectively.

If
p\in\Lambda^{+/-}_{L,\varepsilon}
, then
p
has a
C^{r}
strong-stable/center manifold
W^{ss/c}(p)
. The
local strong-stable/center manifold
W^{ss/c}(p)
at
p
is defined as the connected component of
W^{ss/c}(p)\cap\mathcal{U}_{p}
containing
p
. The
C^{r}
-geometry of
W^{ss/c}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p)
is bounded by
{\bar{L}}
(see Proposition
3.21
). Moreover,
W^{ss/c}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p)
varies
C^{1+\alpha}
-continuously on
p\in\Lambda^{+/-}_{L,\varepsilon}
(see Propositions
3.22
).

By classical Oseledets-Pesin theory, we have
\mu(\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon})\to 1
as
L\to\infty
. However, for the proof of the Main Theorem, we need a much more complete description of the regular blocks of
F
in
\Lambda
. This is provided by the following theorem, which is proved in Subsection
9.3
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Suppose that
F
is
(\varepsilon,\underline{\varepsilon})
-regularly unicritical on its renormalization limit set
\Lambda
. Let
\{c_{m}:=F^{m}(c_{0})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the regular critical orbit of
F
. Denote
\varepsilon^{\prime}:=(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})\varepsilon
. Then there exists a constant
\mathbf{t}\in(0,1)
such that for any
t\in(0,\mathbf{t}]
, we have

i)

\Lambda^{+}_{1/\underline{t},\varepsilon}\supset\displaystyle\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{D}_{c_{-n}}(t\lambda^{\varepsilon^{\prime}n})
,

ii)

\Lambda^{-}_{1/\underline{t},\varepsilon}\supset\displaystyle\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{D}_{c_{n}}(t\lambda^{\varepsilon^{\prime}n})
, and

iii)

\Lambda^{P}_{1/\underline{t},\varepsilon}\supset\displaystyle\Lambda\setminus\bigcup_{m=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbb{D}_{c_{m}}(t\lambda^{\varepsilon^{\prime}|m|})
.

2.2.
Local geometry of the stable and center manifolds

Using Theorem
2.1
, we are able to explicitly describe the local geometry of every strong stable and center manifolds that exist in the system. This description centers around the critical orbit
\{c_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and relies on two key results: Theorem
5.8
and Theorem
9.7
. The first result is about the local dynamics of
F
near
\{c_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
. The second result is about the local geometry of
\Lambda
near
c_{0}
.

In the following discussion, we designate the “vertical” direction as the direction of strong contraction, and the “horizontal” direction as the neutral direction (when such directions are well-defined). We also distinguish
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
as the
critical point
and the
critical value
(the choice of
c_{0}
within the critical orbit can be done arbitrarily).

Consider a cover of the critical orbit by slow exponentially shrinking disks

c_{m}\in\mathbb{D}_{m}:=\mathbb{D}_{c_{m}}(\mathbf{t}\lambda^{\varepsilon^{\prime}|m|})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptm\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.3)

where
\mathbf{t}
is given in Theorem
2.1
. Theorem
5.8
states that under a suitable change of coordinates, the dynamics of
F
on these disks can be uniformized in the following way. On
\mathbb{D}_{m}
with
m\neq 0
, iterates of
F
become
C^{1}
-close to iterates under the linear map
A(x,y)=(x,\lambda y)
. On
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, an application of
F
uniformizes to an application of the Hénon map
H(x,y)=(x^{2}-\lambda y,x)
. See Figure
2
. This is analogous to the one-dimensional case, where for a unimodal map
f
, iterations away from the critical point is effectively linear, while at the critical point,
f
can be uniformized to the power map
P(x)=x^{2}
.

Figure 2.
Uniformization of the local dynamics of
F
near the critical orbit
\{c_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

In the uniformizing coordinates, the local strong stable manifold
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(c_{n})
with
n\geq 1
is a vertical line segment, while the center manifold
W^{c}(c_{-m})
with
m\leq 0
is a horizontal line segment. At the critical moment,
F
maps straight horizontal curves in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
to quadratic vertical curves in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, and
F^{-1}
maps straight vertical curves in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
to quadratic horizontal curves in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
. Observe that the curvature of
W^{c}(c_{n})
for
n\geq 1
increases exponentially fast near
c_{n}
under forward iterates of
F
. Similarly, the curvature of
W^{ss}(c_{-n})
for
n\leq 0
increases exponentially fast near
c_{-n}
under backward iterates of
F
.

Theorem
9.7
states that
\Lambda
“pinches” at
c_{0}
. That is, if
\Phi_{c_{0}}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\Phi_{c_{0}}(\mathbb{D}_{0})
is the change of coordinate on
\mathbb{D}_{0}
that uniformizes the dynamics of
F
, then

\Phi_{c_{0}}(\Lambda\cap\mathbb{D}_{0})\subset T_{0}:=\{(x,y)\;|\;|y|\leq|x|^{1/\varepsilon}\}.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}:=\Phi_{c_{0}}^{-1}(T_{0})
. It follows that
\Lambda\cap\mathbb{D}_{m}
is contained in a pinched region
\mathcal{T}_{c_{m}}
for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that for
n\geq 0
, we have

F^{n}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{-n}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptF^{-(1+n)}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{1+n}})\subset\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}.

We call
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-n}}
the
critical tunnel at
c_{-n}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{c_{1+n}}
the
valuable crescent at
c_{1+n}
. We also show that for all
m,l\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|m|<|l|
we have

\mathcal{T}_{c_{m}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{l}}=\varnothing\hskip 14.22636pt\text{or}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{T}_{c_{m}}\supset\mathcal{T}_{c_{l}}.

The
generation of
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-n}}
is the number of critical tunnels that contain
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-n}}
. Similarly, the
generation of
\mathcal{T}_{c_{1+n}}
is the number of valuable crescents that contain
\mathcal{T}_{c_{1+n}}
.

Lastly, we introduce the vertical manifold

W^{v}(c_{0}):=\Phi_{c_{0}}^{-1}(\{(0,y)\;|\;y\in\mathbb{R}\})\cap\mathbb{D}_{0}.

For
n\geq 0
, define the
vertical manifold
W^{v}(c_{-n})
and the
horizontal manifold
W^{h}(c_{1+n})
as the longest arcs in
\mathbb{D}_{-n}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1+n}
containing
c_{-n}
and
c_{1+n}
respectively such that

F^{n}(W^{v}(c_{-n}))\subset W^{v}(c_{0})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptF^{-(1+n)}(W^{h}(c_{1+n}))\cap\mathbb{D}_{0}\subset W^{v}(c_{0}).

One should consider
W^{v}(c_{-n})
and
W^{h}(c_{1+n})
as “stand ins” for the strong stable and center manifolds for
c_{-n}
and
c_{1+n}
respectively. See Figure
3

Figure 3.
Critical tunnels, valuable crescents, and vertical and horizontal manifolds. The local geometry of the strong-stable manifold of
p_{-i}\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{-i}}
for
i\geq 0
is initially distorted near the critical orbit, but becomes vertical again under the backward dynamics of
F
.

We are now ready to describe the local geometry of all invariant manifolds that exist in
\Lambda
. We begin with strong stable manifolds. Consider a sufficiently small neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{p}
of a uniformly forward regular point
p
. For
k\geq 0
, let
\mathbf{T}_{p}^{k}
be the set of all critical tunnels of generation
k
contained in
\mathcal{U}_{p}
.

If
q\in\mathcal{U}_{p}
is uniformly forward regular, then
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q)
is a proper straight vertical curve in
\mathcal{U}_{p}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{p}^{0}
, then
W^{v}(c_{-m})
is also a proper straight vertical curve in
\mathcal{U}_{p}
. If, on the other hand,
q
is not uniformly forward regular, then
q\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
for some
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{p}^{0}
. See Figure
4
.

Figure 4.
Stable lamination in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{p}
of a uniformly forward regular point
p
. If
q\in\mathcal{U}_{p}
is likewise uniformly forward regular, then
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q)
is proper and vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p}
. If
q
is not uniformly forward regular, then
q\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
for some
m\geq 0
. If
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
is of generation
0
, then
W^{v}(c_{-m})
is also proper and vertical.

To complete our description, we need to understand what local strong stable manifolds look like inside the critical tunnels of generation
0
. Towards this end, let
p=c_{1}
in the above description. For
k\geq 0
, applying
F^{-1}
increases the the generation of critical tunnels in
\mathbf{T}_{c_{1}}^{k}
by one:

\mathbf{T}_{c_{0}}^{k+1}=\{\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m-1}}\;|\;\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{c_{1}}^{k}\}.

Hence, we see that if
q_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{c_{0}}
is not contained in any
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{c_{0}}^{1}
, then
q_{1}\in\mathcal{U}_{c_{1}}
is uniformly forward regular, and
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{1})
is a proper straight vertical curve. Thus, we conclude that

W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{0})\subset F^{-1}(W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{1}))\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}

is a subarc of a quadratic horizontal curve (proper in
\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}
). See Figure
3
. Pulling back under the dynamics of
F
, we obtain the local stable manifolds of every point contained in a critical tunnel of generation
0
, but not contained in a critical tunnel of generation
1
.

This observation also explains the mechanism by which poor forward regularity can improve under the dynamics. By uniformization, we see that iterating by
F^{-1}
on
\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}
is effectively iterating by the linear map
A^{-1}(x,y)=(x,\lambda^{-1}y)
. Under this strong vertical expansion, subarcs of quadratic horizontal curves in
\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}
become straighter and more vertical. So for
q_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}
not contained in any critical tunnels of higher generation, at the moment
-n
when
q_{-n}\notin\mathcal{T}_{c_{-n}}
, the forward regularity at
q_{-n}
is fully recovered to be once again uniform. See Figure
3
.

Figure 5.
Local strong-stable manifold contained in a critical tunnel
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}
of generation
1
(which is the preimage of the critical tunnel
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m+1}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}
of generation
0
).

Proceeding by induction, we obtain a description of the local geometry of the strong stable manifold of every point in
\Lambda
that is contained in at most finitely many critical tunnels. See Figure
5
. We conclude by observing that for
q\in\Lambda
, the deeper the nest of critical tunnels which contains
q
, the worse the forward regularity of
F
at
q
becomes. Consequently, the forward regularity of
F
deteriorates completely at points contained in infinite nests of critical tunnels, and the portions of their stable manifolds that have controlled geometry get reduced to nothing more than the points themselves. The set
\Delta_{c_{0}}^{c}
of all such points has zero measure (see Proposition
6.11
), and is referred to as
the critical dust of
c_{0}
.

Replacing forward regularity with backward regularity, vertical with horizontal, and critical tunnels with valuable crescents in the above discussion, we obtain a description of the local geometry of the center manifold of every point in
\Lambda
except those contained in infinitely many valuable crescents. These exceptional points have completely deteriorated backward regularity. They form a zero-measure set
\Delta_{c_{1}}^{v}
referred to as
the valuable dust of
c_{1}
.

2.3.
Summary of the proof of the main theorem

For
i\in\{0,1\}
, let
\Phi_{c_{i}}:(\mathbb{D}_{i},c_{i})\to(\Phi_{c_{i}}(\mathbb{D}_{i}),0)
be the change of coordinates on
\mathbb{D}_{i}
given in (
2.3
) that uniformizes the local dynamics of
F
along the critical orbit
\{c_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular,

\Phi_{c_{1}}\circ F\circ\Phi_{c_{0}}^{-1}(x,y)=(x^{2}-y,x)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt(x,y)\in\Phi_{c_{0}}(\mathbb{D}_{0}).

(2.4)

In these coordinates,
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(c_{1})
becomes a genuine vertical segment, and
W^{c}(c_{0})
becomes a genuine horizontal segment.

For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{D}^{n}
be the renormalization domain of depth
n
that contains
c_{1}
. A
renormalization piece of depth
n
is the set

\Lambda^{n}_{i}:=\Lambda\cap F^{i-1}(\mathcal{D}^{n})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq i<R_{n}.

Note that
\Lambda^{n}_{R_{n}+1}=\Lambda^{n}_{1}
. Hence,
\Lambda^{n}_{R_{n}}\ni c_{0}
. We show that
\displaystyle\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\Lambda^{n}_{1}=\{c_{1}\}
(see Theorem
12.2
). To simplify our discussion, we will assume that
\Lambda
is totally disconnected (to make the argument work in the general case, we prove that any non-trivial connected component of
\Lambda
is a
C^{r}
-curve that coincides with the center manifold, see Theorem
11.2
).

The main tool used in the proof of the Main Theorem is a structure called a
unicritical cover
\mathcal{C}
of
\Lambda
. It is defined as follows. For any uniformly Pesin regular point
p\in\Lambda
, one can define a
regular chart

\Phi_{p}:(\mathcal{B}_{p},p)\to(B_{p},0)
, which is a
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism from a quadrilateral
\mathcal{B}_{p}\ni p
(called a
regular box at
p
) to a rectangle
B_{p}
with
\|\Phi_{p}^{\pm 1}\|_{C^{r}}=O(1)
,
that satisfies the following properties:

•

The local strong stable manifold
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p)
and the center manifold
W^{c}(p)
map to genuine vertical and horizontal line segments respectively.

•

If there is another uniformly Pesin regular point
p^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that
F(\mathcal{B}_{p})\cap\mathcal{B}_{p^{\prime}}\ni q
, then a horizontal direction at
F^{-1}(q)
(with respect to
\Phi_{p}
) is sent to a sufficiently horizontal direction at
q
(with respect to
\Phi_{p^{\prime}}
) under
DF
. Similarly, a vertical direction at
q
is sent to a sufficiently vertical direction at
F^{-1}(q)
under
DF^{-1}
.

The study of uniformly (partially) hyperbolic systems is greatly facilitated by the fact that the invariant set
can be entirely covered by a finite set of regular boxes, which endows the system with what is referred to as a
local product structure
.

In our setting, any union of regular boxes leaves out points in
\Lambda
that are too close to the critical orbit. To completely cover
\Lambda
, we include critical tunnels and valuable crescents defined in Subsection
2.2
. This leads to the following definition of a
unicritical cover of
\Lambda
:

\mathcal{C}:=\{\mathcal{B}_{p}\}_{p\in\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon}}\cup\{\mathcal{T}_{c_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}

(where
L\geq 1
is some sufficiently large regularity constant). By the compactness of
\Lambda
, we may extract a finite subcover
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset\mathcal{C}
. If
\Lambda
is totally disconnected, then for any
n\geq N
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
such that
\Lambda^{n}_{i}\Subset Q
.

For the Main Theorem, we need to ensure that a unicritical cover
\mathcal{C}_{0}
of
\Lambda
for
F
can fulfill a similar function as local product structures for uniformly (partially) hyperbolic systems. This requires the full use of our understanding of the forward and backward regularity of
F
on
\Lambda
summarized in Subsection
2.2
.

For each
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we endow
\Lambda\cap Q
with a total order induced by the strong-stable lamination as follows. First consider the case when
Q
is either a regular box
\mathcal{B}_{p}
for some uniformly Pesin regular point
p\in\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon}
, or a valuable crescent
\mathcal{T}_{c_{n}}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
q\in\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}\cap Q
is a uniformly forward regular point, then
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q)
is a proper straight vertical curve in
Q
. Hence, the strong-stable lamination clearly induces a total order on
\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}\cap Q
(say, their order of appearance from left to right).

Next, consider the case where
Q
is a critical tunnel
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
for some
m\geq 0
. Let
\Lambda^{+}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}})
be the set of all points
q\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
is the tunnel of the largest generation containing
q
(i.e. there is no deeper tunnel
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-k}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
with
k>m
containing
q
).

If
q_{0}\in\Lambda^{+}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}})
, then its image
q_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}
is not contained in any critical tunnel. Hence,
q_{1}\in\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}
, and
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{1})
is a proper straight vertical curve in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{c_{1}}
of
c_{1}
. This means that, in particular, the component
W^{ss}_{\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}}(q_{1})
of
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{1})\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}
containing
q_{1}
is proper in
\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}
. Define

W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q_{0}):=F^{-1}(W^{ss}_{\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}}(q_{1})).

It is easy to see that the strong-stable lamination

\{W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q)\;|\;F(q)\in\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{1}}\}

induces a total order on
\Lambda^{+}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}})
. Pulling this back by the dynamics, we also obtain a total order on the set
\Lambda^{+}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}})
for any
m\geq 0
.

We now explain how these total orders on separate subsets of
\Lambda
fit together to form a single total order on
\Lambda\cap Q
for any
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Suppose
Q
is either a regular box or a valuable crescent. Let
\mathbf{T}_{Q}^{0}
be the set of critical tunnels in
Q
of generation
0
. For
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{Q}^{0}
, we consider a suitable thin proper nearly-vertical strip
S_{c_{-m}}
that contains
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}
.
Then the collection

\{W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(q)\;|\;q\in\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}\cap Q\}\cup\{S_{c_{-m}}\;|\;\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{Q}^{0}\}

is pairwise disjoint. Hence, the total order on
\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}\cap Q
can be extended to include
\Lambda^{+}(\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}})
with
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\in\mathbf{T}_{Q}^{0}
.

So far, for any
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we have a total order on the set of all points in
\Lambda\cap Q
that are contained in at most one deeper critical tunnel
\mathcal{T}_{c_{-m}}\subset Q
. Proceeding by induction, we obtain a total order on the set of all points in
\Lambda\cap Q
contained in at most finitely many critical tunnels. In other words, we have a total order on the set
(\Lambda\setminus\Delta_{c_{0}}^{c})\cap Q
, where
\Delta_{c_{0}}^{c}
denotes the critical dust of
c_{0}
defined in Subsection
2.2
. Since the intersection of any infinite nest of critical tunnels is a singleton, the total order extends to
\Delta_{c_{0}}^{c}\cap Q
as well. For more details, see Section
10
.

Now that we have the unicritical cover structure
\mathcal{C}_{0}
with well-defined total orders on its elements, we are able to give a general outline of the proof of the Main Theorem. For
n\geq N
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
sufficiently large, let
\mathcal{V}_{1}^{n}
be a suitable thin tubular neighborhood of
W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(c_{1})
in
\mathbb{D}_{1}\ni c_{1}
such that
\mathcal{V}_{1}^{n}\supset\Lambda_{1}^{n}
. Denote
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{n}:=F^{i-1}(\mathcal{V}_{1}^{n})
for
1\leq i<R_{n}
.
We have
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{R_{n}}=F^{R_{n}-1}(\mathcal{V}^{n}_{1})\ni c_{0}
. We need to show that under
F^{R_{n}-1}
:

1)

There is a compression of
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{1}
in the vertical direction by a factor of
\lambda^{(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})R_{n}}
.

2)

A horizontal foliation over
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{1}
is mapped to a horizontal foliation close to
W^{c}(c_{0})
.

Then by applying
F
one more time in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{c_{0}}
of the critical point
c_{0}
, the vertically-compressed, horizontally-aligned rectangular set
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{R_{n}}\subset\mathcal{U}_{c_{0}}
gets bent into the image of a Hénon-like map. See (
2.4
) and Figure
6
.

Figure 6.
An illustration of the statement of the Main Theorem.

There are two crucial differences between the unicritical cover structure
\mathcal{C}_{0}
and the local product structure. The first is that because the covering element
\mathcal{T}_{c_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
at
c_{0}
is pinched, it is a much more delicate problem to ensure that a domain of interest stays contained inside elements in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
under the dynamics. The second is that unlike in the local product structure, horizontal-like and vertical-like directions are not everywhere preserved. Indeed, horizontal directions near
c_{0}
switch to vertical-like directions near
c_{1}
. These difficulties are navigated using the total order on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
, which enables us to prove the following crucial facts.

i)

For
1\leq i<R_{n}
, if
\Lambda^{n}_{i}\subset Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, then the extremal points of
\Lambda^{n}_{i}
with respect to the total order on
\Lambda\cap Q
are
c_{1+i}
and
c_{1+R_{n}+i}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{i}
stay horizontally confined between the strong-stable manifolds of these extremal points.

ii)

We have

\Lambda^{n+1}_{1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{-R_{n}+1}}=\varnothing\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptn\geq N.

iii)

We have

\Lambda^{n+1}_{R_{n}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{c_{R_{n}}}=\varnothing\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptn\geq N.

Facts i) and ii) are used to show that if
\Lambda^{n}_{i}\subset Q\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
for some
1\leq i<R_{n}
, then
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{i}
is contained in a slight thickening
\hat{Q}
of
Q
. Fact iii) is used to show that after passing through the critical value
c_{1}
, horizontal directions in
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{i}
recover their alignment to the local neutral direction before the next closest approach to the critical point
c_{0}
. This means that the vertical directions in
\mathcal{V}^{n}_{i}
typically stay transverse to the neutral direction. Hence, they must contract under the dynamics. The Main Theorem follows. For more details, see Sections
13
and
14
.

2.4.
Outline of the paper

To help the reader, we outline now the main content of each section and we refer to the main result proved in each one. Some of the sections revisit results proved somewhere else.

In Section
3
we present the classical Pesin theory insisting in certain quantitative properties that are needed in our context, in particular, to deal with (forward/backward) “regular finite pieces of orbits” that are not necessary part of regular orbits in the classical sense of Pesin theory. The main result is Theorem
3.6
that provides linearizing coordinates for (finite) regular points.

In Section
4
we revisit a quantitative notion of dissipation that was introduced in
[
CP
]
and proved to be satisfied for odometers in
[
CPT
]
. It allows us to show that the regularity conditions presented in Section
3
are satisfied.

In Section
5
we obtain linearization coordinates at the critical point and value (see Proposition
5.8
) and we estimate the distance of the closest return of the critical point to itself (see Proposition
5.9
).

In the first part of Section
6
we show how the finite (forward/backward) regularity of points in scaled neighborhood of the critical point is controlled (Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
for obtaining regularity and Propositions
6.2
and
6.4
for obstructing regularity). In the second part (Section
6.1
), we introduce the notions of crescents and tunnels (and the scaled ones related to the pre-iterates of the tunnels).

In Section
7
we revisit the notion of mild dissipativity. Recall that this notion states that the stable manifold of any regular point separates the attracting disk where the dynamics is defined. In this section we estimate quantitatively how uniform pieces of local stable manifold separate forward iterates of the attracting disk.

In Section
8
we recall a classical result about the unique ergodicity of odometers and we characterize the trivial connected components of the limit set.

In Section
9
we show that the odometer nearby the critical point is contained inside the pinched tunnel (Theorem
9.7
).

In Section
10
we show how scaled tunnels fits in the regular boxes and in the initial tunnel, providing first a local total order and later a global total order. More precisely, using regular boxes and scaled tunnels, in Section
10.3
we introduce the notion of unicritical cover and we show that such a cover is “totally ordered” (Theorem
10.6
).

3.
Quantitative Pesin Theory

Let
r\geq 2
be an integer. Consider a dissipative
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega
defined on a Jordan domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
\Lambda\Subset\Omega
be a totally invariant compact set, and let
\mu
be an ergodic probability measure supported on
\Lambda
. Suppose that the Lyapunov exponents of
F
with respect to
\mu
are
0
and
\log\lambda<0
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be a small constant such that
{\bar{\varepsilon}}<1
.

3.1.
Definitions of regularity

Let
N,M\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0,\infty\}
and
L\geq 1
. A point
p\in\Lambda
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E^{+}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
if for
s\in\{-r,r-1\}
, we have

L^{-1}\lambda^{(1+\varepsilon)n}\leq\frac{(\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F^{n})^{s}}{\|DF^{n}|_{E^{+}_{p}}\|^{s-1}}\leq L\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq N.

(3.1)

Similarly,
p
is
M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E^{-}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
if for
s\in\{-r,r-1\}
, we have

L^{-1}\lambda^{-(1-\varepsilon)n}\leq\frac{(\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F^{-n})^{s}}{\|DF^{-n}|_{E^{-}_{p}}\|^{s-1}}\leq L\lambda^{-(1+\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for all}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq M.

(3.2)

If (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) are both satisfied with
E_{p}^{v}:=E_{p}^{+}=E_{p}^{-}
, then we say that
p
is
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}
. If additionally, we have
M=N=\infty
, then we say that
p
is
Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}
.

We say that
p
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
\tilde{E}_{p}^{+}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
if for
s\in\{-r+1,r\}
, we have

L^{-1}\lambda^{(1+\varepsilon)n}\leq\frac{\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F^{n}}{\|D_{p}F^{n}|_{\tilde{E}_{p}^{+}}\|^{s+1}}\leq L\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq N.

(3.3)

Similarly, we say that
p
is
M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
\tilde{E}_{p}^{-}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
if for
s\in\{-r+1,r\}
, we have

L^{-1}\lambda^{-(1-\varepsilon)n}\leq\frac{\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F^{-n}}{\|D_{p}F^{-n}|_{\tilde{E}_{p}^{-}}\|^{s+1}}\leq L\lambda^{-(1+\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq M.

(3.4)

If both (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) hold with
E_{p}^{h}:=\tilde{E}_{p}^{+}=\tilde{E}_{p}^{-}
, then
p
is
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}
. If, additionally, we have
M=N=\infty
, then
p
is
Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}
.

Proposition 3.1

(Vertical forward regularity
=
horizontal forward regularity)
.

Suppose
p
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
. Let
E_{p}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}\setminus\{E_{p}^{v}\}
. If
\measuredangle(E_{p}^{v},E_{p}^{h})>\theta
, then the point
p
is
N
-times forward
({\bar{L}}/\theta^{2},\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}
.

Conversely, if
p
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
, then there exists
E_{p}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
such that
p
is
N
-times forward
({\bar{L}},{\bar{\varepsilon}})_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}
.

Proposition 3.2

(Horizontal backward regularity
=
vertical backward regularity)
.

Suppose
p
is
M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
. Let
E_{p}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}\setminus\{E_{p}^{h}\}
. If
\measuredangle(E_{p}^{h},E_{p}^{v})>\theta
, then the point
p
is
M
-times backward
({\bar{L}}/\theta^{2},\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}
.

Conversely, if
p
is
M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
, then there exists
E_{p}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
such that
p
is
M
-times backward
({\bar{L}},{\bar{\varepsilon}})_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}
.

Proposition 3.3

(Pesin regularity
=
vertical forward regularity
+
horizontal backward regularity
+
transversality)
.

Suppose
p
is
N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
and
M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
with
\theta:=\measuredangle(E_{p}^{v},E_{p}^{h})>0
. Let
\mathcal{L}\geq 1
be the minimum value such that
p
is
(M,N)
-times
(\mathcal{L},{\bar{\varepsilon}})_{v}
-regular along
E_{p}^{v}
and
(\mathcal{L},{\bar{\varepsilon}})_{h}
-regular along
E_{p}^{h}
. Then we have
k/\theta^{2}<\mathcal{L}<{\bar{L}}/\theta^{2}
for some uniform constant
k>0
.

Suppose
p_{0}\in\Lambda
is
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{0}}^{v/h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{0}}
. Define the
regularity
of
p_{m}
as the smallest value
\mathcal{L}_{p_{m}}\geq 1
such that
p_{m}
is
(M+m,N-m)
-times
(\mathcal{L}_{p_{m}},\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{m}}^{v/h}
.

Proposition 3.4

(Decay in regularity)
.

For
-M\leq m\leq N
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{p_{m}}<L^{2}\lambda^{-2\varepsilon|m|}.

Remark 3.5
.

By Proposition
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
, we see that regularity along a vertical direction and regularity along a horizontal direction are interchangeable conditions (at the cost of increasing the constants of regularity by some uniform amount). Henceforth, the vertical direction will be taken as the default unless otherwise stated.

3.2.
Linearization along regular orbits

For the remainder of this section, we assume that
p_{0}\in\Lambda
is
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p_{0}}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{0}}
for some
M,N,\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
and
L\geq 1
.

For
l,w>0
, denote

\mathbb{I}(l):=(-l,l)\subset\mathbb{R},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{B}(l,w):=\mathbb{I}(l)\times\mathbb{I}(w)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{B}(l):=\mathbb{B}(l,l).

Theorem 3.6
.

For
-M\leq m\leq N
, let

l_{p_{m}}:={\bar{L}}^{-1}\lambda^{{\bar{\varepsilon}}|m|}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptU_{p_{m}}:=\mathbb{B}(l_{p_{m}}).

Then there exists a
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi_{p_{m}}:(\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}},p_{m})\to(U_{p_{m}},0)
such that

\|\Phi_{p_{m}}^{\pm 1}\|_{C^{r}}=O({\bar{L}}\lambda^{-{\bar{\varepsilon}}|m|}),\hskip 14.22636ptD\Phi_{p_{m}}(E^{v}_{p_{m}})=E^{gv}_{0},

and
\Phi_{p_{n+1}}\circ F|_{\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}}\circ\Phi_{p_{m}}^{-1}
extends to a globally defined
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism
F_{p_{m}}:(\mathbb{R}^{2},0)\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},0)
satisfying the following properties.

i)

We have
\displaystyle\|F_{p_{m}}^{\pm 1}\|_{C^{r}}=O(1)
.

ii)

The map
F_{p_{m}}
is uniformly
C^{1}
-close to

D_{0}F_{p_{m}}=A_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{m}&0\\
0&b_{m}\end{bmatrix},

with
\lambda^{1+{\bar{\varepsilon}}}<b_{m}<\lambda^{1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}}
and
\lambda^{\bar{\varepsilon}}<a_{m}<\lambda^{-{\bar{\varepsilon}}}
.

iii)

We have

F_{p_{m}}(x,y)=(f_{p_{m}}(x),e_{p_{m}}(x,y))\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
f_{p_{m}}:(\mathbb{R},0)\to(\mathbb{R},0)
is a
C^{r}
-diffeomorphism, and
e_{p_{m}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{r}
-map with
e_{p_{m}}(\cdot,0)\equiv 0
.

The construction in Theorem
3.6
is referred to as
a linearization of
F
along the
(M,N)
-orbit of
p_{0}
with vertical direction
E^{v}_{p_{0}}
. For
-M\leq m\leq N
, we refer to
l_{p_{m}}
,
\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}
,
\Phi_{p_{m}}
and
F_{p_{m}}
as a
regular radius
, a
regular neighborhood
, a
regular chart
and a
linearized map at
p_{m}
respectively. For
1\leq n\leq N-m
, we denote

F_{p_{m}}^{n}:=F_{p_{m+n-1}}\circ\ldots\circ F_{p_{m+1}}\circ F_{p_{m}}.

Let
X_{m}\subset U_{p_{m}}
be a set. We denote

X_{m+l}:=F_{p_{m}}^{l}(X_{m})

for all
l\in\mathbb{Z}
for which the right-hand side is well-defined. Similarly, for any direction
E_{z_{m}}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{z_{m}}
at a point
z_{m}\in U_{p_{m}}
, we denote

E_{z_{m+l}}:=DF_{p_{m}}^{l}(E_{z_{m}}).

Remark 3.7
.

Note that we have the freedom to make the regular radii used in Theorem
3.6
uniformly smaller whenever necessary (i.e. replace
l_{p_{m}}
by
\underline{l_{p_{m}}}
for all
-M\leq m\leq N
).

Proposition 3.8
.

For
-M\leq m\leq N
, we have

\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}})\asymp{\bar{L}}^{-1}\lambda^{{\bar{\varepsilon}}|m|}.

The following result states that restricted to the regular neighborhoods, iterates of
F
are nearly linear.

Proposition 3.9

(Linear dynamics inside regular neighborhoods)
.

For any constant
k>0
, the regular radii in Theorem
3.6
can be chosen sufficiently small so that the following holds. Let
-M\leq m\leq N
and
-M-m\leq l\leq N-m
. Suppose that
q_{m+i}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{m+i}}
for
i\in[m,m+l]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.
Write
z_{m}:=\Phi_{p_{m}}(q_{m})\in U_{p_{m}}
. Then for all
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

\|D_{z_{m}}F_{p_{m}}^{l}(v)-D_{0}F_{p_{m}}^{l}(v)\|<k\|D_{0}F_{p_{m}}^{l}(v)\|

and

\|D_{q_{m}}F^{l}(v)-D_{p_{m}}F^{l}(v)\|<k\|D_{p_{m}}F^{l}(v)\|.

Moreover,

1-k<\frac{\operatorname{Jac}_{z_{m}}F_{p_{m}}^{l}}{\operatorname{Jac}_{0}F_{p_{m}}^{l}},\frac{\operatorname{Jac}_{q_{m}}F^{l}}{\operatorname{Jac}_{p_{m}}F^{l}}<1+k.

3.3.
Vertical and horizontal directions

For
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, denote the genuine vertical/horizontal direction at
z
by
E^{gv/gh}_{z}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{z}
respectively.

Let
-M\leq m\leq N
. For
q\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}
, write
z:=\Phi_{p_{m}}(q)
. The
vertical/horizontal direction at
q
in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}
is defined as
E^{v/h}_{q}:=D\Phi_{p_{m}}^{-1}(E^{gv/gh}_{z})
. For
t\geq 0
, we say that
E_{q}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{q}
is
t
-vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
if

\frac{\measuredangle(E_{q},E_{q}^{v/h})}{\measuredangle(E_{q},E_{q}^{h/v})}<t.

Similarly, we say that a
C^{1}
-curve
\gamma\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
is
t
-vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
if

\frac{\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}(s),E_{\gamma(s)}^{v/h})}{\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}(s),E_{\gamma(s)}^{h/v})}<t\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pts\in(0,|\gamma|).

By the construction of regular charts in Theorem
3.6
, vertical directions are invariant under
F
:

\text{i.e.}\hskip 14.22636ptDF(E_{q}^{v})=E_{F(q)}^{v}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptq\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}.

Note that the same is not true for horizontal directions. However, the following result states that they are still nearly invariant under
F
.

Proposition 3.10

(Near invariance of horizontal directions)
.

Let
-M\leq m\leq N
and
-M-m\leq l\leq N-m
. Suppose that
q_{m+i}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{m+i}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pti\in[m,m+l]\cap\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\tilde{E}_{q_{m+l}}^{h}:=D_{q_{m}}F^{l}(E_{q_{m}}^{h})
.
Write
z_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m}):=\Phi_{p_{m}}(q_{m})
and

\tilde{E}_{z_{m+l}}^{h}:=D_{z_{m}}F_{p_{m}}^{l}(E_{z_{m}}^{gh})=D_{q_{m+l}}\Phi_{p_{m+l}}(\tilde{E}_{q_{m+l}}^{h}).

Then we have

\measuredangle(\tilde{E}_{z_{m+l}}^{h},E_{z_{m+l}}^{gh}),\measuredangle(\tilde{E}_{q_{m+l}}^{h},E_{q_{m+l}}^{h})<K|y_{m+l}|^{1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}}

for some uniform constant
K>1
.

Proposition 3.11

(Vertical alignment of forward contracting directions)
.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
and
\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{q_{0}}
. Suppose for some
0<n\leq N
, we have
q_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{i}}
for
0\leq i\leq n
. If
\nu:=\|DF^{n}|_{\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{v}}\|<{\bar{L}}^{-1}\lambda^{{\bar{\varepsilon}}n}
,
then

\measuredangle(\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{v},E_{q_{0}}^{v})<{\bar{L}}\lambda^{-{\bar{\varepsilon}}n}\nu+{\bar{L}}\lambda^{(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})n}.

Proposition 3.12

(Horizontal alignment of backward neutral directions)
.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
and
\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{q_{0}}
. Suppose for some
0<m\leq M
, we have
q_{-i}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{-i}}
for
0\leq i\leq m
. If
\mu:=\|DF^{-m}|_{\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{h}}\|<{\bar{L}}^{-1}\lambda^{-(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})m}
, then

\measuredangle(\tilde{E}_{q_{0}}^{h},E_{q_{0}}^{h})<{\bar{L}}\lambda^{(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})m}(1+\mu).

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{D}\to T^{1}\mathcal{D}
be a unit vector field on a domain
\mathcal{D}\subset\Omega
. Define

DF_{*}(\mathcal{E})(p):=\frac{DF(\mathcal{E}(p))}{\|DF(\mathcal{E}(p))\|}\in T^{1}_{F(p)}F(\mathcal{D})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptp\in\mathcal{D}.

If
\mathcal{E}(p)
is
t
-vertical for all
p\in\mathcal{D}\cap\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
, then
\mathcal{E}
is said to be
t
-vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
.

For
-N\leq m\leq N
, define
\mathcal{E}^{v}_{p_{m}}:\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}\to T^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}})
to be the unit vector field given by

\mathcal{E}^{v}_{p_{m}}(q)\in E^{v}_{q}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptq\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}.

Proposition 3.13

(Vertical alignment of non-horizontal vector fields under backward iterates)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
be a domain, and
0\leq n\leq N
. Suppose

\mathcal{D}_{i}:=F^{i}(\mathcal{D}_{0})\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{i}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq i\leq n.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{D}_{n}\to T^{1}(\mathcal{D}_{n})
be a
C^{r-1}
-unit vector field. If
\mathcal{E}
is
t
-vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{n}}
for some
t\geq 0
, then we have

\|DF^{-n}_{*}(\mathcal{E})-\mathcal{E}^{v}_{p_{0}}|_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\|_{C^{r-1}}\leq(1+t^{2})(1+\|\mathcal{E}\|_{C^{r-1}}){\bar{L}}\lambda^{(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})n}.

Let
\gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a
C^{r}
-curve. We define

\|\gamma\|_{C^{r}}:=\|\phi_{\gamma}\|_{C^{r}},

where
\phi_{\gamma}:[0,|\gamma|]\to\gamma
is the parameterization of
\gamma
by its arclength.

Proposition 3.14

(Horizontal alignment of non-vertical curves under forward iterates)
.

Let
\gamma_{0}\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
be a
C^{r}
-curve, and
0\leq n\leq N
. Suppose

\gamma_{i}:=F^{i}(\gamma_{0})\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{i}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt0\leq i\leq n.

If
\gamma_{0}
is
t
-horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
for some
t\geq 0
, then
\Phi_{p_{n}}(\gamma_{n})
is the horizontal graph of some
C^{r}
-map
g_{n}:J_{n}\subset\mathbb{I}(l_{p_{n}})\to\mathbb{I}(l_{p_{n}})
with

\|g_{n}\|_{C^{r}}\leq(1+t^{r+1})\|\gamma_{0}\|_{C^{r}}{\bar{L}}\lambda^{(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})n}.

3.4.
Truncated and pinched regular neighborhoods

Let
\delta\in({\bar{\varepsilon}},1)
be a small constant such that
{\bar{\delta}}<1
. For
n\geq 0
, the
n
-times
\delta
-truncated regular neighborhood of
p_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}^{\delta n}:=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}\left(U_{p_{0}}^{\delta n}\right)\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636ptU_{p_{0}}^{\delta n}:=\mathbb{B}\left(\lambda^{\delta n}l_{p_{0}},l_{p_{0}}\right).

(3.5)

The purpose of truncating a regular neighborhood is to ensure that its iterated images stay inside regular neighborhoods.

Lemma 3.15
.

For
0\leq n\leq N
, we have

F^{i}(\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}^{{\bar{\varepsilon}}n})\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{i}}
for
0\leq i\leq n
.

For
\omega>1
and
{t}\in(0,l_{p_{0}})
, define

T_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t}):=\{(x,y)\in U_{p_{0}}\;|\;|x|<{t}\;\text{and}\;|y|<|x|^{\omega}\}.

We refer to

\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t}):=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(T_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t}))\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}

as a
\omega
-pinched regular neighborhood of length
{t}
at
p_{0}
. Note that
p_{0}\not\in\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t})
. For
n\geq 0
, we also denote

T_{p_{0}}^{\omega,\delta n}({t}):=T_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t})\cap U_{p_{0}}^{\delta n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega,\delta n}({t}):=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(T_{p_{0}}^{\omega,\delta n}({t}))=\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}({t})\cap\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}^{\delta n}.

If the length
{t}
is not indicated, then it is assumed to be equal to
l_{p_{0}}
(i.e.
\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}:=\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}(l_{p_{0}})
).

The purpose of pinching a regular neighborhood is to ensure that its iterated preimages stay inside regular neighborhoods.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
0\leq n\leq M
. If
\omega>(1+{\bar{\varepsilon}})/\delta
, then
F^{-i}(\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega,\delta n})\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{-i}}
for
0\leq i\leq n
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\omega\in(1,{\bar{\varepsilon}}^{-1}]
. For
q_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}\setminus\{p_{0}\}
, write
z_{0}=(x_{0},y_{0}):=\Phi_{p_{0}}(q_{0})\in U_{p_{0}}
. If
q_{-n}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{-n}}
for some

n>(1+{\bar{\varepsilon}})\omega\log_{\lambda}|x_{0}|,

then
q_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{p_{0}}^{\omega}
.

3.5.
Regular boxes

Let
L^{\prime}
be a constant sufficiently uniformly larger than
L
. In this subsection, we assume that

M,N>\log_{\lambda}\overline{L^{\prime}}.

(3.6)

A
regular box at
p_{0}
is defined as

\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}:=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(B_{p_{0}})\Subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636ptB_{p_{0}}:=\mathbb{B}(1/L^{\prime}).

Denote

\mathbb{L}^{h}_{b}:=\{(x,b)\;|\;x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

The
top-bottom boundary of
\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
is defined as

\hat{\partial}\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}:=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(\hat{\partial}B_{p_{0}}),\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636pt\hat{\partial}B_{p_{0}}:=\partial B_{p_{0}}\cap(\mathbb{L}^{h}_{1/L^{\prime}}\cup\mathbb{L}^{h}_{-1/L^{\prime}}).

A Jordan curve
\Gamma:(0,1)\to\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
is said to be
vertically proper in
\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
if
\Gamma
extends continuously to
[0,1]
, and
\Gamma(0)
and
\Gamma(1)
are in distinct components of
\hat{\partial}\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
. More generally, a Jordan curve
\Gamma
in
\Omega
is said to be vertically proper in
\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
if
\Gamma\cap\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
is.

Let
q\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
. We say that
E_{q}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{q}
is
sufficiently vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
if
E_{q}
is
1/\overline{L^{\prime}}
-vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
. Similarly, we say that a
C^{1}
-curve
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
is
sufficiently vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
if
\Gamma
is
1/\overline{L^{\prime}}
-vertical/horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
.

Proposition 3.18

(Consistent vertical and horizontal directions inside regular boxes)
.

Let
\tilde{M},\tilde{N}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0,\infty\}
. Suppose
\tilde{p}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{p_{0}}
is
(\tilde{M},\tilde{N})
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
\tilde{E}^{v}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
. Denote the vertical/horizontal direction and the local vertical/horizontal manifold at
\tilde{p}_{0}
associated with the linearization of
F
along the
(\tilde{M},\tilde{N})
-orbit of
\tilde{p}_{0}
with vertical direction
\tilde{E}^{v}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
by
\tilde{E}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}^{v/h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
and
\tilde{W}^{v/h}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\tilde{p}_{0})\subset\mathcal{U}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
respectively.

i)

If
\tilde{N}>\log_{\lambda}\overline{L^{\prime}}
, then
\tilde{E}^{v}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
and
\tilde{W}^{v}(\tilde{p}_{0})
are sufficiently vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
.

ii)

If
\tilde{M}>\log_{\lambda}\overline{L^{\prime}}
, then
\tilde{E}^{h}_{\tilde{p}_{0}}
and
\tilde{W}^{h}(\tilde{p}_{0})
are sufficiently horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
.

Proposition 3.19

(Verticality and horizontality of curves preserved inside regular boxes)
.

For
0\leq k\leq K<\infty
, let
p^{k}\in\Omega
be
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E^{v}_{p^{k}}
. If
\Gamma_{0}\subset\Omega
is a
C^{1}
-curve such that

\Gamma_{k}\subset\mathcal{B}_{p^{k}}
for all
0\leq k\leq K
, then the following holds.

i)

If
\Gamma_{0}
is sufficiently horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p^{0}}
, then
\Gamma_{k}
is sufficiently horizontal in
\mathcal{U}_{p^{k}}
for all
0\leq k\leq K
. If, additionally,
\Gamma_{0}
is
C^{r}
and
\|\Gamma_{0}\|_{C^{r}}<{\bar{L}}
,
then
\|\Gamma_{K}\|_{C^{r}}<{\bar{L}}
.

ii)

If
\Gamma_{K}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{q_{K}}
is sufficiently vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p^{K}}
, then
\Gamma_{k}
is sufficiently vertical in
\mathcal{U}_{p^{k}}
for all
0\leq k\leq K
. Moreover,

|\Gamma_{0}|>{\bar{L}}^{-1}|\Gamma_{K}|.

(3.7)

If, additionally,
\Gamma_{K}
is
C^{r}
and
\|\Gamma_{K}\|_{C^{r}}<{\bar{L}}
, then
\|\Gamma_{0}\|_{C^{r}}<{\bar{L}}
.

3.6.
Stable and center manifolds

If
N=\infty
, then Proposition
3.11
implies that
E_{p_{0}}^{v}
is the unique direction along which
p_{0}
is infinitely forward
(L,\varepsilon,\lambda)
-regular. In this case, we denote

E_{p_{0}}^{ss}:=E_{p_{0}}^{v},

and refer to this direction as the
strong stable direction at
p_{0}
. Moreover, we define the
local strong stable manifold at
p_{0}
as

W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p_{0}):=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(\{(0,y)\in U_{p_{0}}\}),

and the
strong stable manifold at
p_{0}
as

W^{ss}(p_{0}):=\{q\in\Omega\;|\;F^{n}(q)\in W^{ss}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p_{m})\;\;\text{for some}\;\;n\geq 0\}.

If
M=\infty
, we denote

E_{p_{0}}^{c}:=E_{p_{0}}^{h},

and refer to this direction as the
center direction at
p_{0}
. Moreover, we define the
(local) center manifold at
p_{0}
as

W^{c}(p_{0}):=\Phi_{p_{0}}^{-1}(\{(x,0)\in U_{p_{0}}\}).

Unlike strong-stable manifolds, center manifolds are not uniquely and canonically defined. However, the following result states that it still has a canonical jet.

Proposition 3.20

(Canonical
C^{r}
-jets of center manifolds)
.

Suppose
M=\infty
. Let
\Gamma_{0}:(-t,t)\to\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
be a
C^{r}
-curve parameterized by its arclength such that
\Gamma_{0}(0)=p_{0}
, and for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\|DF^{-n}|_{\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(t)}\|<\lambda^{-(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}})n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt|t|<\lambda^{\varepsilon n}.

Then
\Gamma_{0}
has a degree
r
tangency with
W^{c}(p_{0})
at
p_{0}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

If
p
is infinite-time forward/backward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular. Then

\left\|W^{ss/c}_{\operatorname{loc}}(p)\right\|_{C^{r}}<{\bar{L}}.

Proposition 3.22
.

The strong stable manifold
W^{ss}(p)
depends
C^{r}
-continuously on
p\in\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}
. The center manifold
W^{c}(p)
depends
C^{r-{\bar{\varepsilon}}}
-continuously on
p\in\Lambda^{-}_{L,\varepsilon}
.

Remark 3.23
.

Henceforth, we say that
p
is infinite-time forward/backward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular (without specifying
v
/
h
in the subscript) if
p
is infinite-time forward/backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E^{ss/c}_{p}
. Furthermore, we say that
p
is Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular if
p
is infinite-time forward and backward
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular, and we have
\measuredangle(E^{ss}_{p},E^{c}_{p})>1/L
(see Proposition
3.3
). The sets of all infinite-time forward/backward/Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular points in
\Lambda
are referred to as the
forward, backward
and
Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)
-regular blocks
, and are denoted by
\Lambda^{+}_{L,\varepsilon}
,
\Lambda^{-}_{L,\varepsilon}
and
\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon}
respectively.

Proposition 3.24
.

We have
\mu(\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon})\to 1
as
L\to\infty
.

4.
Homogeneity

Let
F
be the diffeomorphism considered in Section
3
. For
\eta\in(0,1)
with
\bar{\eta}<1
, we say that
F
is
\eta
-homogeneous on
\Lambda
if for all
p\in\Lambda
and
E_{p}\in\mathbb{P}_{p}^{2}
, we have:

i)

\lambda^{1+\eta}<\|D_{p}F|_{E_{p}}\|<\lambda^{-\eta}
and

ii)

\lambda^{1+\eta}<\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F<\lambda^{1-\eta}
.

Homogeneity considerably simplifies the regularity conditions given in (
3.1
), (
3.2
), (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). Let
N,M\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0,\infty\}
;
L\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(\bar{\eta},1)
with
{\bar{\varepsilon}}<1
. Then a point
p\in\Lambda
is:

•

N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E^{v}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
if

\|DF^{n}|_{E^{v}_{p}}\|\leq L\lambda^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq N;

•

M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E^{v}_{p}
if

\|DF^{-n}|_{E^{v}_{p}}\|\geq L^{-1}\lambda^{-(1-\varepsilon)n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq M;

•

N
-times forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E^{h}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
if

\|DF^{n}|_{E^{h}_{p}}\|\geq L^{-1}\lambda^{\varepsilon n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq N;

•

M
-times backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{h}
-regular along
E^{h}_{p}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p}
if

\|DF^{-n}|_{E^{h}_{p}}\|\leq L\lambda^{-\varepsilon n}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636pt1\leq n\leq M.

Proposition 4.1
.

Suppose
F|_{\Lambda}
is uniquely ergodic. Then for any
\eta>0
, there exists
N=N(\eta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
n\geq N
, then the map
F^{n}
is
\eta
-homogeneous on
\Lambda
.

4.1.
Pliss moments

Let
\eta,\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
with
\bar{\eta}<\varepsilon<\delta
and
{\bar{\delta}}<1
.
Suppose that
F
is
\eta
-homogeneous on
\Lambda
. We show that for such maps, regularity is a statistically common occurrence.

Let
p_{0}\in\Lambda
. Suppose that along its forward/backward/full orbit,
p_{0}
exhibits asymptotically regular behavior with marginal exponent
\varepsilon
(in the sense made rigorous in the propositions stated below). Loosely speaking, an integer
m
is a Pliss moment for the orbit of
p_{0}
if
p_{m}
is
(1,\delta)
-regular.

Proposition 4.2

(Frequency of Pliss moments along forward orbit)
.

Let
p_{1}\in\Lambda
. Suppose there exist
E_{p_{1}}^{v}
or
E_{p_{1}}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{1}}
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{n}|_{E_{p_{1}}^{v}}\|>1-\varepsilon

or

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{n}|_{E_{p_{1}}^{h}}\|<\varepsilon.

Let
\{n_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{N}
be the increasing set of all moments such that
p_{n_{i}}
is
(n_{i},\infty)
-times
(1,\delta)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{n_{i}}}^{v/h}
. Then

\liminf_{i\to\infty}\frac{i}{n_{i}}>1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta

If
p_{1}
is infinitely forward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{1}}^{v/h}
for some
L\geq 1
, then

i>\left(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta\right)n_{i}+\delta^{-1}\log_{\lambda}L\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptn_{i}>\left(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta\right)n_{i+1}+\delta^{-1}\log_{\lambda}L.

Proposition 4.3

(Frequency of Pliss moments along backward orbit)
.

Let
p_{-1}\in\Lambda
. Suppose there exists
E_{p_{-1}}^{v}
or
E_{p_{-1}}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{-1}}
such that

\liminf_{m\to-\infty}\frac{1}{m}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{m}|_{E_{p_{-1}}^{v}}\|>1-\varepsilon

or

\limsup_{m\to-\infty}\frac{1}{m}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{m}|_{E_{p_{-1}}^{h}}\|<\varepsilon.

Let
\{-m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset-\mathbb{N}
be the decreasing set of all moments such that
p_{-m_{i}}
is
(\infty,m_{i})
-times
(1,\delta)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{-m_{i}}}^{v/h}
. Then

\liminf_{i\to\infty}\frac{i}{m_{i}}>1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta.

If
p_{-1}
is infinitely backward
(L,\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{-1}}^{v/h}
for some
L\geq 1
, then

i>(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta)m_{i}+\delta^{-1}\log_{\lambda}L\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptm_{i}>(1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta)m_{i+1}+\delta^{-1}\log_{\lambda}L.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
p_{0}\in\Lambda
be Pesin
(L,\varepsilon)_{v/h}
-regular along
E^{v/h}_{p_{0}}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{0}}
, and let

n>-\delta^{-1}\log_{\lambda}{\bar{L}}.

If
p_{n}
is
(n,\infty)
-times
(1,\underline{\delta})_{v/h}
-regular along
E^{v/h}_{p_{n}}
, then
p_{n}
is Pesin
(1,\delta)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{n}}^{v/h}
. Similarly, if
p_{-n}
is
(\infty,n)
-times
(1,\underline{\delta})_{v/h}
-regular along
E^{v/h}_{p_{-n}}
, then
p_{-n}
is Pesin
(1,\delta)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{-n}}^{v/h}
.

Proposition 4.5

(Frequency of Pliss moments along full orbit)
.

Let
p_{0}\in\Lambda
. Suppose there exists
E_{p_{0}}^{v}
or
E_{p_{0}}^{h}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{0}}
such that

\liminf_{|m|\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{m}|_{E_{p_{0}}^{v}}\|>1-\varepsilon

or

\limsup_{|m|\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\log_{\lambda}\|DF^{m}|_{E_{p_{0}}^{h}}\|<\varepsilon.

Let
\{m_{i}\}_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{Z}
be the increasing set of all moments with

m_{n}\geq 0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptm_{-n}\leq 0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptn\geq 0,

such that
p_{m_{i}}
is Pesin
(1,\delta)_{v/h}
-regular along
E_{p_{m_{i}}}^{v/h}
. Then

\liminf_{|i|\to\infty}\frac{i}{m_{i}}>1-{\bar{\varepsilon}}/\delta.

The numbers
n_{i}
in Proposition
4.2
,
-m_{i}
in Proposition
4.3
and
m_{i}
in Proposition
4.5
are referred to as
Pliss moments
.

Define

\Lambda^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{L\geq 1}\Lambda^{\pm}_{L,\varepsilon}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\Lambda^{P}_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{L\geq 1}\Lambda^{P}_{L,\varepsilon}.

Note that these sets are totally invariant. Also, recall that
\Lambda^{\pm}_{\operatorname{Ly}}
and
\Lambda^{P}_{\operatorname{Ly}}
denote the set of forward/backward and Pesin Lyapunov regular points in
\Lambda
respectively.

Proposition 4.6
.

We have

\Lambda^{\pm}_{\operatorname{Ly}}\subset\Lambda^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}
and
\Lambda^{P}_{\operatorname{Ly}}\subset\Lambda^{P}_{\varepsilon}.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
p_{0}\in\Lambda
be a Lyapunov forward/backward/Pesin regular point. Then there exists
N=N(p_{0})\geq 0
such that for
n\geq N
, the point
p_{0}
is infinitely forward/backward/Pesin
(1,\varepsilon)
-regular for the map
F^{n}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Suppose that
\mu
is the unique invariant probability measure for
F|_{\Lambda}
. Then

\mu(\Lambda^{\pm}_{1,\varepsilon})>\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+\eta}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mu(\Lambda^{P}_{1,\varepsilon})>\frac{\varepsilon-\eta}{\varepsilon+\eta}.

5.
Critical Orbits

Let
F:\Omega\to F(\Omega)\Subset\Omega
be the diffeomorphism considered in Section
3
(not necessarily homogeneous). For
p\in\Lambda
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
r_{p}:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\theta_{p}:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
be given by

r_{p}(t)(\cos(2\pi\theta_{p}(t)),\sin(2\pi\theta_{p}(t))):=D_{p}F(\cos(2\pi t),\sin(2\pi t)).

For

E_{p}^{t}=\{l(\cos(2\pi t),\sin(2\pi t))\;|\;l\in\mathbb{R}\}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p},

the
projective derivative of
F
at
E_{p}^{t}
is defined as

\partial_{\mathbb{P}}F(E_{p}^{t}):=\theta_{p}^{\prime}(t)=\frac{\operatorname{Jac}_{p}F}{\|D_{p}F|_{E_{p}^{t}}\|^{2}}.

Definition 5.1
.

A point
c\in\Lambda
is a
(general) critical point
if there exists a tangent direction
E_{c}^{*}\in\mathbb{P}_{c}^{2}
such that

\partial_{\mathbb{P}}F^{\pm n}(E_{c}^{*})\geq 1\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptn\in\mathbb{N}.

In this case,
E_{c}^{*}
is referred to as a
(general) critical direction at
c
.

Related to critical point, the following theorem is proved in
[
CP2
]
.

Theorem 5.2
.

The set
\Lambda
admits a dominated splitting if and only if it does not contain any critical point of
F
.

Remark 5.3
.

In particular, if
F|_{\Lambda}
is dissipative, it follows that it contains a critical point of
F
if and only if
F|_{\Lambda}
is not uniformly partially hyperbolic. Moreover, as explained in
[
CPT
]
, since odometers can not be partially hyperbolic, then they has to contain (general) critical points.

5.1.
Critical points obstruct linearization

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be a small constant such that
{\bar{\varepsilon}}<1
.

Proposition 5.4

(No critical point in regular neighborhoods)
.

Let
p_{0}\in\Lambda
be
(M,N)
-times
(L,\varepsilon)_{v}
-regular along
E_{p_{0}}^{v}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}_{p_{0}}
for some
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
and
L\geq 1
. Let

\{\Phi_{p_{m}}:\mathcal{U}_{p_{m}}\to U_{p_{m}}\}_{m=-M}^{N}

be a linearization of the
(M,N)
-orbit of
p_{0}
with vertical direction
E_{p_{0}}^{v}
. Suppose there exists a point
q_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{p_{0}}
such that
